I… yeah, I do. Not every second the same way, but it keeps coming back that I’m the reason everything got worse.

Like my husband—if I hadn’t been so… me, maybe he wouldn’t have gone downhill. It’s like I poisoned everything I touched.

So when I think about dying, it feels like… maybe that’s what I deserve. Like whatever happens to me is just what I earned.

I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.

Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.

It’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.

I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.

I remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.

So… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”

Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.

Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.

And then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.

Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.

There was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.3, F33.3, F32.2, F33.2, F43.10, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I keep having these thoughts that maybe it’d be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like, not have to face everybody. And then I start thinking maybe dying would be fair, like punishment for what I’ve done to my family and the guys I work with.\n\nI know that sounds… I don’t know. I do flooring, and when something’s cut wrong you can’t always fix it, you just see the mistake every time you walk in the room. That’s kind of how it feels with me lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die because of what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I do. Not every second the same way, but it keeps coming back that I’m the reason everything got worse.\n\nLike my husband—if I hadn’t been so… me, maybe he wouldn’t have gone downhill. And with the crew, jobs got messed up, people lost work, and then I think about clients’ houses where things didn’t come out right, and I can still see the seams in my head, the bad cuts, the places that weren’t level. It’s like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo when I think about dying, it feels like… maybe that’s what I deserve. Like whatever happens to me is just what I earned.\n\nDoctor: Are you absolutely certain that you deserve to die as punishment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “absolutely” like… like I could prove it in court or something. But it feels true. It keeps coming back.\n\nI keep thinking I’m the reason my husband got worse, and the crew lost work, and those clients’ houses got messed up. I’ll be doing something normal, like looking at a floor in a grocery store, and I start seeing all the places I’ve ruined things. Like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo… certain? Maybe not every minute. But when it hits, it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe as a fact that you have ruined other people’s lives?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.3, F32.3, F32.2, F33.2, F43.10, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I keep having these thoughts that maybe it’d be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like, not have to face everybody. And then I start thinking maybe dying would be fair, like punishment for what I’ve done to my family and the guys I work with.\n\nI know that sounds… I don’t know. I do flooring, and when something’s cut wrong you can’t always fix it, you just see the mistake every time you walk in the room. That’s kind of how it feels with me lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die because of what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I do. Not every second the same way, but it keeps coming back that I’m the reason everything got worse.\n\nLike my husband—if I hadn’t been so… me, maybe he wouldn’t have gone downhill. And with the crew, jobs got messed up, people lost work, and then I think about clients’ houses where things didn’t come out right, and I can still see the seams in my head, the bad cuts, the places that weren’t level. It’s like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo when I think about dying, it feels like… maybe that’s what I deserve. Like whatever happens to me is just what I earned.\n\nDoctor: Are you absolutely certain that you deserve to die as punishment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “absolutely” like… like I could prove it in court or something. But it feels true. It keeps coming back.\n\nI keep thinking I’m the reason my husband got worse, and the crew lost work, and those clients’ houses got messed up. I’ll be doing something normal, like looking at a floor in a grocery store, and I start seeing all the places I’ve ruined things. Like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo… certain? Maybe not every minute. But when it hits, it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe as a fact that you have ruined other people’s lives?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.

It’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.

But when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.

But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. I’ll be doing something normal, like looking at a floor in a grocery store, and I start seeing all the places I’ve ruined things. Like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo… certain? Maybe not every minute. But when it hits, it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe as a fact that you have ruined other people’s lives?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.\n\nIt’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. There was a stretch, a few weeks maybe, where I was getting up more normal, eating a little better, going to work and doing what I was supposed to do.\n\nBut the voice was still there at work. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. I was getting up, eating a little better, going to work.\n\nBut I still heard him at work. Like I’d be doing the flooring, trying to get the edges right, and then I’d hear the voice. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad with exact dates, because jobs blur together, especially when you’re going from one house to another and everyone wants the flooring done yesterday.\n\nBut there was definitely a stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t depressed like that—like I was getting up, eating a little better, not crying all day—and I was still hearing him at work. I’d be on a job, trying to focus, and the voice would still come from behind me. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.

Sometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow. But feeling great on no sleep? I can’t say I’ve noticed that.

I don’t think of myself that way. Energized maybe sometimes, but that could just be when a job has to get done and you’re running around because the tiles are late or somebody measured wrong, and then you’re the one trying to fix it.\n\nEuphoric sounds like… happy in a big way? I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear time like that coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.\n\nSometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow. But feeling great on no sleep? I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this current period, have you had little interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have things I actually looked forward to. I’d paint sometimes, or cook these big meals—like not just heating something up, I mean the whole thing, chopping stuff, making a mess in the kitchen. I used to run before jobs too, if I had time, which sounds insane now because flooring already beats up your body.

And theater rehearsals… I used to show up for those. I liked being part of it, even if I was tired. Now it all feels like… chores from somebody else’s life. Like I know those were my things, but I don’t feel anything for them. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or just eat whatever’s easiest, I don’t really care. It’s just… gone flat.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I keep having these thoughts that maybe it’d be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like, not have to face everybody. And then I start thinking maybe dying would be fair, like punishment for what I’ve done to my family and the guys I work with.\n\nI know that sounds… I don’t know. I do flooring, and when something’s cut wrong you can’t always fix it, you just see the mistake every time you walk in the room. That’s kind of how it feels with me lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die because of what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I do. Not every second the same way, but it keeps coming back that I’m the reason everything got worse.\n\nLike my husband—if I hadn’t been so… me, maybe he wouldn’t have gone downhill. And with the crew, jobs got messed up, people lost work, and then I think about clients’ houses where things didn’t come out right, and I can still see the seams in my head, the bad cuts, the places that weren’t level. It’s like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo when I think about dying, it feels like… maybe that’s what I deserve. Like whatever happens to me is just what I earned.\n\nDoctor: Are you absolutely certain that you deserve to die as punishment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “absolutely” like… like I could prove it in court or something. But it feels true. It keeps coming back.\n\nI keep thinking I’m the reason my husband got worse, and the crew lost work, and those clients’ houses got messed up. I’ll be doing something normal, like looking at a floor in a grocery store, and I start seeing all the places I’ve ruined things. Like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo… certain? Maybe not every minute. But when it hits, it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe as a fact that you have ruined other people’s lives?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.\n\nIt’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. There was a stretch, a few weeks maybe, where I was getting up more normal, eating a little better, going to work and doing what I was supposed to do.\n\nBut the voice was still there at work. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. I was getting up, eating a little better, going to work.\n\nBut I still heard him at work. Like I’d be doing the flooring, trying to get the edges right, and then I’d hear the voice. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad with exact dates, because jobs blur together, especially when you’re going from one house to another and everyone wants the flooring done yesterday.\n\nBut there was definitely a stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t depressed like that—like I was getting up, eating a little better, not crying all day—and I was still hearing him at work. I’d be on a job, trying to focus, and the voice would still come from behind me. And I was still convinced the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the orders or materials or whatever. So… yeah, it was at least around a month, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t know. I don’t think of myself that way. Energized maybe sometimes, but that could just be when a job has to get done and you’re running around because the tiles are late or somebody measured wrong, and then you’re the one trying to fix it.\n\nEuphoric sounds like… happy in a big way? I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear time like that coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.\n\nSometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I keep having these thoughts that maybe it’d be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like, not have to face everybody. And then I start thinking maybe dying would be fair, like punishment for what I’ve done to my family and the guys I work with.\n\nI know that sounds… I don’t know. I do flooring, and when something’s cut wrong you can’t always fix it, you just see the mistake every time you walk in the room. That’s kind of how it feels with me lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you truly deserve to die because of what you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I do. Not every second the same way, but it keeps coming back that I’m the reason everything got worse.\n\nLike my husband—if I hadn’t been so… me, maybe he wouldn’t have gone downhill. And with the crew, jobs got messed up, people lost work, and then I think about clients’ houses where things didn’t come out right, and I can still see the seams in my head, the bad cuts, the places that weren’t level. It’s like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo when I think about dying, it feels like… maybe that’s what I deserve. Like whatever happens to me is just what I earned.\n\nDoctor: Are you absolutely certain that you deserve to die as punishment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “absolutely” like… like I could prove it in court or something. But it feels true. It keeps coming back.\n\nI keep thinking I’m the reason my husband got worse, and the crew lost work, and those clients’ houses got messed up. I’ll be doing something normal, like looking at a floor in a grocery store, and I start seeing all the places I’ve ruined things. Like I poisoned everything I touched.\n\nSo… certain? Maybe not every minute. But when it hits, it feels like I deserve whatever happens to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe as a fact that you have ruined other people’s lives?\n\nPatient: I mean… it feels like a fact, yeah. Maybe if someone laid it all out on paper, I don’t know, maybe they’d say there were other reasons. But in my head it’s me.\n\nMy husband got worse and I keep going back over everything I did wrong, every time I wasn’t enough. And the crew—when jobs went bad, people lost work, and I think, that started with me. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.\n\nIt’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. There was a stretch, a few weeks maybe, where I was getting up more normal, eating a little better, going to work and doing what I was supposed to do.\n\nBut the voice was still there at work. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. I was getting up, eating a little better, going to work.\n\nBut I still heard him at work. Like I’d be doing the flooring, trying to get the edges right, and then I’d hear the voice. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad with exact dates, because jobs blur together, especially when you’re going from one house to another and everyone wants the flooring done yesterday.\n\nBut there was definitely a stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t depressed like that—like I was getting up, eating a little better, not crying all day—and I was still hearing him at work. I’d be on a job, trying to focus, and the voice would still come from behind me. And I was still convinced the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the orders or materials or whatever. So… yeah, it was at least around a month, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t know. I don’t think of myself that way. Energized maybe sometimes, but that could just be when a job has to get done and you’re running around because the tiles are late or somebody measured wrong, and then you’re the one trying to fix it.\n\nEuphoric sounds like… happy in a big way? I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear time like that coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.\n\nSometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow. But feeling great on no sleep? I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you had little interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have things I actually looked forward to. I’d paint sometimes, or cook these big meals—like not just heating something up, I mean the whole thing, chopping stuff, making a mess in the kitchen. I used to run before jobs too, if I had time, which sounds insane now because flooring already beats up your body.\n\nAnd theater rehearsals… I used to show up for those. I liked being part of it, even if I was tired. Now it all feels like… chores from somebody else’s life. Like I know those were my things, but I don’t feel anything for them. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or just eat whatever’s easiest, I don’t really care. It’s just… gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually irritable for days in a row?\n\nPatient: I mean… irritable, sure, everybody gets irritable, right? Especially when you’re working around dust and grout and somebody keeps stepping where you just told them not to step. That can set me off.\n\nBut for days in a row in some unusual way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I can point to a stretch like that exactly. Sometimes people annoy me and I’m short with them, but then there’s usually a reason, like work stuff or noise or just being worn out. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I think about a year and five months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after my husband hurt his back and ended up mostly stuck at home. That changed everything in the house, you know? Like the rhythm of the day, what I had to do, what I could leave for later… and somehow it all just kept going. I don’t think it ever really let up since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: At work I've caught myself pacing the same hallway with my trowel in my hand, checking the grout lines over and over, then freezing in a doorway ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s gotten a lot worse. I don’t like saying that, because flooring is one of those things where you’re supposed to know what you’re doing and just keep it moving, you know? Like if the cuts are wrong or the crew’s waiting on you, everything backs up. And clients always want to change something right when you’ve got adhesive open—sorry, that’s not really the point.

I had to stop leading installations. I couldn’t keep track of the crew right, or talk to clients the way I needed to, or make the calls on the job without second-guessing and messing things up. My apprentice started stepping in before I ruined work, and my husband too, even with his back, he’d try to catch things before I put somebody at risk or made a bad decision. So… yeah. Daily responsibilities, work, all of it—way worse than before.

That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.\n\nIt’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. There was a stretch, a few weeks maybe, where I was getting up more normal, eating a little better, going to work and doing what I was supposed to do.\n\nBut the voice was still there at work. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. I was getting up, eating a little better, going to work.\n\nBut I still heard him at work. Like I’d be doing the flooring, trying to get the edges right, and then I’d hear the voice. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad with exact dates, because jobs blur together, especially when you’re going from one house to another and everyone wants the flooring done yesterday.\n\nBut there was definitely a stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t depressed like that—like I was getting up, eating a little better, not crying all day—and I was still hearing him at work. I’d be on a job, trying to focus, and the voice would still come from behind me. And I was still convinced the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the orders or materials or whatever. So… yeah, it was at least around a month, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t know. I don’t think of myself that way. Energized maybe sometimes, but that could just be when a job has to get done and you’re running around because the tiles are late or somebody measured wrong, and then you’re the one trying to fix it.\n\nEuphoric sounds like… happy in a big way? I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear time like that coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.\n\nSometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow. But feeling great on no sleep? I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you had little interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have things I actually looked forward to. I’d paint sometimes, or cook these big meals—like not just heating something up, I mean the whole thing, chopping stuff, making a mess in the kitchen. I used to run before jobs too, if I had time, which sounds insane now because flooring already beats up your body.\n\nAnd theater rehearsals… I used to show up for those. I liked being part of it, even if I was tired. Now it all feels like… chores from somebody else’s life. Like I know those were my things, but I don’t feel anything for them. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or just eat whatever’s easiest, I don’t really care. It’s just… gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually irritable for days in a row?\n\nPatient: I mean… irritable, sure, everybody gets irritable, right? Especially when you’re working around dust and grout and somebody keeps stepping where you just told them not to step. That can set me off.\n\nBut for days in a row in some unusual way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I can point to a stretch like that exactly. Sometimes people annoy me and I’m short with them, but then there’s usually a reason, like work stuff or noise or just being worn out. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I think about a year and five months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after my husband hurt his back and ended up mostly stuck at home. That changed everything in the house, you know? Like the rhythm of the day, what I had to do, what I could leave for later… and somehow it all just kept going. I don’t think it ever really let up since then.\n\nDoctor: Since all this started, has your ability to work and handle daily responsibilities gotten a lot worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten a lot worse. I don’t like saying that, because flooring is one of those things where you’re supposed to know what you’re doing and just keep it moving, you know? Like if the cuts are wrong or the crew’s waiting on you, everything backs up. And clients always want to change something right when you’ve got adhesive open—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI had to stop leading installations. I couldn’t keep track of the crew right, or talk to clients the way I needed to, or make the calls on the job without second-guessing and messing things up. My apprentice started stepping in before I ruined work, and my husband too, even with his back, he’d try to catch things before I put somebody at risk or made a bad decision. So… yeah. Daily responsibilities, work, all of it—way worse than before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I keep having these thoughts that maybe it’d be better if I just didn’t wake up. Like, not have to face everybody. I do flooring, and when something’s cut wrong you can’t always fix it, you just see the mistake every time you walk in the room. Even clients’ homes, I can picture the messed-up spots like I’m standing there again, the seams, the edges… and then I start thinking about how I used to check the tile rows over and over, like if one was crooked the whole room looked ruined.\n\nSo yeah, I believe I ruined things for people. Like I poisoned everything I touched, and whatever happens to me after that… I guess I earned it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe this is definitely true, even if other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the hard part. People can tell me it’s not true, and I’ll nod, I’ll try to listen, but then I see the floor pattern or I get a text from a supplier, or somebody calls about a permit, and it all lines up again.\n\nIt’s not just when I’m crying in bed, either. I can be up, doing something normal, and suddenly I’m sure the homeowners and suppliers are watching me through those things—like checking whether I’m ruining jobs on purpose. And once I see it that way, nobody can really talk me out of it.\n\nI know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels definite when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period of depression that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to count it. Like, I’ve had bad stretches before, but I don’t know if they were two weeks exactly or if I’m just mixing them together.\n\nI remember times where I’d be low and everything felt heavy, but then there were jobs and measurements and people needing answers, and the days kind of blur. Sometimes a whole house would take longer than it should because the subfloor wasn’t level, and then I’d lose track of whether I was sad the whole time or just exhausted from dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can’t say clearly, “yes, that was two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: When this is happening, do you actually believe the homeowners and suppliers are watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not all the time in a normal way, like if you ask me right now I can hear how strange it sounds, but when it’s happening, yes, I believe it.\n\nIt’ll be something like the floor pattern lining up wrong, or a text from a supplier coming in right after I had that thought, or a permit call, and then it feels like they’re connected. Like the homeowners and suppliers are using those things to keep track of me, to see if I’m messing jobs up on purpose.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about past jobs, like the tiles in that one kitchen where the light hit the edge weird—sorry, that’s not what you asked. But yes. In those moments, I’m certain, and people telling me “that’s not happening” doesn’t really get through.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed but still believed the homeowners or suppliers were watching you through those things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were times like that. That’s what makes it confusing, because it wasn’t always when I was at the very bottom.\n\nThere was a stretch for several weeks where I was getting up more, eating a little better, not just crying all day. I could go to work and do the normal stuff—well, normal for flooring, checking edges and seams and all that—and I still heard the voice at work. And I still felt sure the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow.\n\nLike even if I wasn’t completely depressed that day, I’d see something or hear something and it would all click into place again. So… yes, it happened even when I wasn’t as depressed.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of it in a clean way, like “yes, that happened for two weeks,” but my memory doesn’t line up like that.\n\nThere were definitely times before where I was down a lot, like most of the day maybe, but then I’d be going to work or dealing with a job that ran over because the tile order was wrong, and I’d sort of just push through. And then I can’t tell if it was depression every day or just the stress and being worn out.\n\nSo… I can’t really say for sure that there was another full two-week stretch like that before this.\n\nDoctor: When you hear the voice at work, is it coming from outside your head, like an actual voice speaking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It sounds like it’s outside my head. Like an actual man’s voice, usually behind me at a job site, or sometimes in my kitchen.\n\nIt’ll say things like, “You missed it,” or “Everyone knows,” and it’s clear enough that I turn around expecting someone to be standing there. Like maybe one of the guys came back in, or the homeowner walked in without me hearing the door, which happens on some sites because everyone’s coming and going and the saw’s loud and—sorry.\n\nBut when I turn around, there’s nobody there. It doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels like it came from behind me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not depressed at all but still heard that man's voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wasn’t crying all day then. There was a stretch, a few weeks maybe, where I was getting up more normal, eating a little better, going to work and doing what I was supposed to do.\n\nBut the voice was still there at work. Like I’d be focused on a seam or checking the edges, and then I’d hear him. And I still thought the clients and suppliers were somehow tracking me, like through the job stuff, orders, materials… I know that sounds like a lot, but it was there even when I wasn’t feeling completely depressed.\n\nDoctor: During the times when you were not depressed at all, did you still hear the man’s voice?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s what I mean, I think. There was this stretch, maybe several weeks, where I wasn’t… I wasn’t crying all day or stuck in bed or anything. I was getting up, eating a little better, going to work.\n\nBut I still heard him at work. Like I’d be doing the flooring, trying to get the edges right, and then I’d hear the voice. And I still felt like the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the job stuff or the orders. Even though I wasn’t feeling as depressed then, that part didn’t really stop.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time when you heard the man's voice for at least one month while you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m bad with exact dates, because jobs blur together, especially when you’re going from one house to another and everyone wants the flooring done yesterday.\n\nBut there was definitely a stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t depressed like that—like I was getting up, eating a little better, not crying all day—and I was still hearing him at work. I’d be on a job, trying to focus, and the voice would still come from behind me. And I was still convinced the clients and suppliers were tracking me somehow, through the orders or materials or whatever. So… yeah, it was at least around a month, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t know. I don’t think of myself that way. Energized maybe sometimes, but that could just be when a job has to get done and you’re running around because the tiles are late or somebody measured wrong, and then you’re the one trying to fix it.\n\nEuphoric sounds like… happy in a big way? I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear time like that coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt full of energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really like “full of energy.” I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep much, but then I’m usually dragging the next day, especially if I have to be on my knees doing flooring or lifting boxes.\n\nSometimes I stay up because my mind won’t settle, or I’m thinking about a job, like whether the measurements were right or if the supplier sent the wrong color again—because that happens, and then everyone acts like it’s your fault somehow. But feeling great on no sleep? I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you had little interest or pleasure in things you usually care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I mean, I used to have things I actually looked forward to. I’d paint sometimes, or cook these big meals—like not just heating something up, I mean the whole thing, chopping stuff, making a mess in the kitchen. I used to run before jobs too, if I had time, which sounds insane now because flooring already beats up your body.\n\nAnd theater rehearsals… I used to show up for those. I liked being part of it, even if I was tired. Now it all feels like… chores from somebody else’s life. Like I know those were my things, but I don’t feel anything for them. If I miss rehearsal or don’t paint or just eat whatever’s easiest, I don’t really care. It’s just… gone flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually irritable for days in a row?\n\nPatient: I mean… irritable, sure, everybody gets irritable, right? Especially when you’re working around dust and grout and somebody keeps stepping where you just told them not to step. That can set me off.\n\nBut for days in a row in some unusual way… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I can point to a stretch like that exactly. Sometimes people annoy me and I’m short with them, but then there’s usually a reason, like work stuff or noise or just being worn out. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely been more than six months. I think about a year and five months now, if I’m counting right.\n\nIt kind of started after my husband hurt his back and ended up mostly stuck at home. That changed everything in the house, you know? Like the rhythm of the day, what I had to do, what I could leave for later… and somehow it all just kept going. I don’t think it ever really let up since then.\n\nDoctor: Since all this started, has your ability to work and handle daily responsibilities gotten a lot worse than it used to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten a lot worse. I don’t like saying that, because flooring is one of those things where you’re supposed to know what you’re doing and just keep it moving, you know? Like if the cuts are wrong or the crew’s waiting on you, everything backs up. And clients always want to change something right when you’ve got adhesive open—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nI had to stop leading installations. I couldn’t keep track of the crew right, or talk to clients the way I needed to, or make the calls on the job without second-guessing and messing things up. My apprentice started stepping in before I ruined work, and my husband too, even with his back, he’d try to catch things before I put somebody at risk or made a bad decision. So… yeah. Daily responsibilities, work, all of it—way worse than before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so unusually energized or driven that other people thought you were acting unlike yourself?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized… I don’t know. I’ve had days where I pushed through because the job had to get done, like when you’ve got a whole room torn up and the client’s furniture is sitting in the hallway and you can’t just leave it like that. So I can look driven, I guess.\n\nBut people thinking I was acting unlike myself because I had all this energy? I’m not sure. Nobody’s really said that to me that I remember. Mostly if someone comments, it’s because I’m not keeping up right, not because I’m too energized.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
